## Reasoning

The champion scores -4.20, which is significantly better than all others (next best is -0.10). The champion uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74, yellow LED) with 4 transitions: neighbor-below-8, perceive-yellow(clr4), gray-floor, neighbor-below-1
- State 1: GoToColor toward GREEN (clr=2, which is green) with 3 transitions: neighbor-below-8, black-floor(low prob), black-floor(higher prob)

The key insight: robots explore, then when they detect yellow or few neighbors, go toward the green source area (scoring -1 per item). The black floor triggers returning... wait, actually GoToColor clr=2 is GREEN. When on black floor they transition back to exploration. This seems to be collecting green items (which decrease score = good).

**Strategy:**
1. Survive: copy champion exactly
2. Exploit: small variations of champion (tweak probabilities, thresholds, add/modify transitions)
3. Explore: try different topologies - e.g., 3-state machines with explicit nest-return behavior, trying GoToColor toward RED wall (to return to nest), etc.

Let me think more carefully about the mission:
- GREEN side items decrease score by 1 (good!)
- BLUE side items increase score by 1 (bad!)
- So robots should: go to GREEN source → get item → return to nest (WHITE floor)

The champion seems to work by going to GREEN source (GoToColor clr=2=green), then returning to exploration when on black floor. The nest has WHITE floor. Perhaps when on black floor (source area), they collected item, then go back to exploring toward nest.

Let me generate 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2-6. Small exploitations of champion
7-14. Explorations with different topologies

For exploitations, I'll tweak:
- rwm0: 74 → 60, 85
- p0x2 (gray prob): 0.953 → 0.8, 0.99
- clr1: 2 (green) → keep green, it's working
- transition probabilities in state 1

For explorations:
- 3-state machines: explore → go to green → go to red wall (nest)
- Try GoToColor clr=2 in state 0 and GoAwayColor clr=3 (blue)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The top machines are clearly converging on a 2-state design:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm ~60-85, yellow LED) with 4 transitions: neighbor-below-8 (w≈6.86), perceive-yellow (p≈0.12), gray-floor (p≈0.95), neighbor-below-1 (w≈17.4)
- State 1: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2) with 3 transitions: neighbor-below-8 (w≈10.6), black-floor (p≈0.09), black-floor (p≈0.25)

Key observations:
- Champion: rwm=85, score -4.34
- Rank 2: rwm=74, score -4.32 (very close)
- Rank 3: rwm=60, score -4.32 (very close)
- The topology is very stable and robust

The transitions from state 0 all point to state 1 (the only other state), and vice versa. The black-floor transitions from state 1 back to state 0 make sense: when the robot lands on black (source area), it might want to go back to explore/return.

Wait - actually looking at the champion more carefully: ALL transitions from state 0 go to destination 0, which with 2 states means they go to state 1 (the other state). And all transitions from state 1 go to destination 0 = state 0. So both states transition to the other state.

Let me focus exploitation on:
1. Tweaking rwm (try 90, 100, 70)
2. Tweaking the w parameters for neighbor conditions
3. Tweaking the black-floor probabilities
4. Adding/modifying transitions

For exploration:
- Try white-floor condition to trigger GoToColor green (detect nest arrival, go back to source)
- Try 3-state with explicit return behavior

## Reasoning

The new champion (-4.84) is a simplified 2-state machine:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=74, yellow LED) with 3 transitions: perceive-yellow (p=0.1174), black-floor (p=0.8), neighbor-below-2 (w=15)
- State 1: GoToColor GREEN (clr=2) with 3 transitions: neighbor-below-8 (w=10.59), white-floor (p=0.85), black-floor (p=0.15)

Key insight: The champion uses **white-floor** (C=2) to return from GoToColor back to exploration - this means when robots reach the NEST (white floor) after collecting a green item, they go back to exploring. And black-floor triggers return from source exploration. The neighbor-below-2 condition causes isolated robots to go toward green source.

The champion is simpler (3 transitions each state) vs the previous champion's 4 transitions. The perceive-yellow (clr=4) triggers GoToColor green - robots see yellow LEDs of other robots and join them? Or is clr=4 something else? Color 4 in the arena context... yellow is LED color 4 on robots. So perceiving yellow-LED robots triggers going to green source.

Key parameters to exploit:
- rwm: 74 is good (vs 85 which scores slightly worse)
- p0x0 (perceive-yellow prob): 0.1174
- p0x1 (black-floor prob): 0.8
- p0x2 neighbor threshold: 2, w=15
- p1x1 (white-floor prob): 0.85
- p1x2 (black-floor prob): 0.15

Strategy:
1. Survive: copy champion
2. Exploit: vary rwm (50, 65, 80), vary p0x1 (0.7, 0.9), vary neighbor threshold (1, 3), vary white-floor prob (0.75, 0.95), vary w0x2 (10, 20)
3. Explore: add 4th transition, try different color perceptions (green clr=2), try GoToColor RED (nest wall)

## Reasoning

The champion is now -5.50 with rwm=60. The pattern is clear:
- rwm trend: 85→74→65→60→55 (still improving as we go lower)
- The topology is very stable: 2-state, same structure

Key observations:
1. rwm=60 beats rwm=65 beats rwm=68, and rwm=55 is rank 2. Need to try lower values (40, 45, 50)
2. p0x1 (black-floor prob): 0.8 wins over 0.9. Try 0.75, 0.7
3. p1x1 (white-floor prob): 0.85 wins over 0.95 and 0.90. The original 0.85 seems optimal
4. w0x2 (steepness for neighbor-below-2): 15 wins over 13 and 17. Try 16, 14
5. Adding white-floor transition to state 0 (4th transition with p=0.6) scored -5.22 - interesting!
6. The core parameters p0x0=0.1174, l0x0=4, p0x2=2, w1x0=10.5944, p1x2=0.15 seem stable

**Exploitation:**
- Try rwm: 40, 45, 50, 55, 57 (approaching lower)
- Combine rwm=60 with p0x1=0.75 or 0.85
- Try the white-floor 4th transition with rwm=60
- Try w0x2=16 or 14

- Try combining lower rwm with white-floor trigger in state 0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Major breakthrough!** The champion jumped from -5.50 to -12.73 by changing state 1 from **GoToColor (B=8)** to **GoAwayColor (B=9)** with clr=2 (GREEN). This is a huge discovery!

**Understanding the champion:**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=60, yellow LED) → transitions to state 1 when: perceiving yellow robots (p=0.1174), on black floor (p=0.8), or few neighbors (below-2, w=15)
- State 1: **GoAwayColor GREEN (B=9, clr=2)** → transitions back to state 0 when: few neighbors (below-8, w=10.59), on white floor (p=0.85), on black floor (p=0.15)

**Why does GoAwayColor GREEN work so well?** When a robot is near the green source (black floor), GoAway from green means it moves AWAY from green... but wait, that would mean moving toward the nest (white floor). So the robot: explores → reaches green area (black floor triggers) → GoAway from green = moves toward nest → delivers green item → white floor triggers return to exploration. This is actually a complete foraging loop!

The gap between rank 1 (-12.73) and rank 2 (-5.27) is enormous. I need to:
1. **Survive**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploit heavily**: Vary parameters around the champion's B=9 configuration
   - Try different rwm values (50, 55, 65, 70)
   - Try different p0x1 (black-floor prob): 0.7, 0.85, 0.9
   - Try different p1x1 (white-floor prob): 0.75, 0.90, 0.95
   - Try different w1x0 (neighbor threshold steepness): 8, 12
   - Try GoAwayColor with clr=3 (blue - avoid blue items)
3. **Explore**: Add 4th transitions, try 3-state versions

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
